Even when my mind is racing with all those ideas, I still know what's real and what isn't.\n\nDoctor: To be absolutely sure, have you ever experienced any moments during these high-energy periods where you felt you possessed special powers, had a mission of great importance that others didn't understand, or felt like your thoughts were being broadcast to others?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt like I had literal superpowers, a grand mission, or that my thoughts were being broadcast. The closest I get is feeling like I have this \"special grind mode\" where I convince myself I can build an app or launch a channel and fix all our money problems overnight. But I know it’s not an actual superpower, just me getting overly confident.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy episodes that last for a week, does the intensity of your symptoms cause a marked impairment in your social or occupational functioning, or are they manageable enough that you can still carry out your daily responsibilities?"

They definitely cause major problems, because I end up snapping at my wife and talking over her, and I've blown past important work deadlines and let our bills pile up.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy episodes, have you ever required hospitalization, or did you experience any features like losing touch with reality, such as hearing voices or holding beliefs that others found clearly impossible?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been hospitalized during those high-energy times, and I've never experienced anything like hearing voices or losing touch with reality. Even when my mind is racing with all those ideas, I still know what's real and what isn't.\n\nDoctor: To be absolutely sure, have you ever experienced any moments during these high-energy periods where you felt you possessed special powers, had a mission of great importance that others didn't understand, or felt like your thoughts were being broadcast to others?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt like I had literal superpowers, a grand mission, or that my thoughts were being broadcast. The closest I get is feeling like I have this \"special grind mode\" where I convince myself I can build an app or launch a channel and fix all our money problems overnight. But I know it’s not an actual superpower, just me getting overly confident.\n\nDoctor: During these high-energy episodes that last for a week, does the intensity of your symptoms cause a marked impairment in your social or occupational functioning, or are they manageable enough that you can still carry out your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: While I'm in them, I feel totally fine and convinced I can handle everything, but they aren't actually manageable. I get so scattered starting multiple projects and jumping from task to task without finishing anything. This disorganization, along with the crashes that follow, makes me blow past deadlines and lose track of our bills.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these high-energy states, is the impairment in your functioning severe enough that you are unable to work or maintain your social relationships for the duration of the episode, or are you still able to function at a basic level?\n\nPatient: During those high phases, I actually feel like I'm functioning at a super-high level—I'm constantly busy messaging people, planning projects, and doing basic tasks, so I'm not completely incapacitated. But it still causes real problems because I start a million things without finishing them, snap at my wife over budget questions, and ultimately wind up blowing past deadlines anyway."

I'm still able to get out of bed and work on projects during those up times, which is completely different from my low periods where I shut down entirely and can't function at all."

I only came in now because the constant cycling and my recent deep lows are completely ruining our finances and my job search.\n\nDoctor: To clarify the degree of impairment, during these high-energy episodes, would you say your functioning is unequivocally different from your usual self, but still allows you to maintain your job and social responsibilities without the level of disruption that would typically require hospitalization?\n\nPatient: Yes, my behavior is definitely very different from my normal self during those times—my wife notices it immediately because I'm so amped up, talking a mile a minute, and barely sleeping. While I've never needed hospital care and can still technically function day-to-day, it still causes real problems because I take on way too much, never finish projects, and constantly snap at my wife."

Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
